I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I mean, “not true” is a hard thing, because in an office people call things unrealistic all the time when what they really mean is inconvenient or not in the budget yet.

Like, I’d suggest something and people would say, “That’s not how we do things,” but that’s not the same as it being false, right? HR is full of that — policies that everyone treats like laws of nature, and then six months later leadership changes and suddenly the impossible thing is mandatory.

So… I don’t think I believed anything obviously untrue. At least not that I remember. But if you mean did other people think I was being unrealistic about my plans, maybe, yes, they probably did. I’m not sure that’s the same thing though.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m trying to think before the work situation, and I don’t know if I’d call it the same, exactly. But there have definitely been stretches where I get this… drive, like I can’t just sit and wait for things to happen.

Like I’ll get in early, before most people are even settled, and I’m already walking the floors, checking in on what teams are doing, looking at conference rooms like I’m mapping a campaign or something. Then I’m back rewriting decks that were probably fine the night before, sending emails to people because some piece of the plan suddenly seems obvious, rearranging timelines, moving things around.

It’s not even always that I feel stressed. It’s more like my body has a motor in it. Sitting at my desk feels almost impossible, like I should be up fixing something or connecting dots. Which… in HR, that can look productive, honestly. People like when you’re proactive. But yes, I guess there have been times like that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF30.1, F31.1, F60.3, F60.8, F43.9, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because things kind of blew up at work. I work in HR, and there was this onboarding launch we’d been talking about forever, and I just… pushed it ahead. I committed company money and vendor time before all the approvals were actually in place.\n\nAt the time it felt like the right move, like bold and necessary, you know? Like if I didn’t drive it forward, it would just die in meetings like everything else. But now I’m on leave, and people keep asking me what I was thinking, and honestly I’m not sure how to answer that without sounding ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: During the time you pushed the project ahead, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly. I just wasn’t paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not “happy” exactly, not like vacation happy. More like I felt brilliantly alive, like I could finally show leadership what HR is supposed to be, not just paperwork and policy reminders.\n\nAnd then if someone questioned me — even something small, like an edit to a deck or a routine budget question — I’d get furious. Like, disproportionate furious. Which is strange because normally I can sit through endless feedback meetings, that’s basically half of HR, but during those weeks it felt like everyone was trying to slow me down or make me smaller.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel driven to take on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… it didn’t feel like “a lot” at the time, it felt like finally doing what should have been done already.\n\nI was trying to run the HR redesign, and at the same time I was reaching out to gallery partners — which, I know, sounds unrelated, but there was this idea about making the onboarding experience more connected to local culture, and then that made me think of festivals, so I started contacting festival organizers too. And then there were these staff conflicts that had been sitting around forever, so I was jumping into those like, “I can fix this today.”\n\nIt was like every idea had to be acted on immediately, before anyone could bury it in another committee meeting.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel unusually powerful or especially certain that your ideas were uniquely important?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision. If you don’t sound convinced, then everyone piles on with doubts and suddenly you’re in a meeting about a meeting.\n\nThe “uniquely important” part… I don’t know. I remember feeling like people weren’t seeing the obvious path, but that happens in HR more than people realize. Everyone thinks HR is just forms and complaints, but there’s strategy there, culture, retention, all of it. Like when we changed the performance review templates years ago, everyone acted like it was just a document, but it changed the whole tone of manager conversations.\n\nSo… maybe I felt strongly that my ideas mattered, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually powerful. I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, were you sleeping much less than usual and still feeling full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Sleep is one of those things I only notice after the fact, if that makes sense. I don’t keep track of it very well.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches where I’m up late thinking about work or answering one more email, but I don’t know that I could say, “Yes, I was sleeping much less and felt great the next day.” I’d have to think about it more. My wife would probably have a better read on that than I do — she notices things like whether I’m getting up at weird hours or moving around the house.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no exactly, I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you believe things that other people said were not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “not true” is a hard thing, because in an office people call things unrealistic all the time when what they really mean is inconvenient or not in the budget yet.\n\nLike, I’d suggest something and people would say, “That’s not how we do things,” but that’s not the same as it being false, right? HR is full of that — policies that everyone treats like laws of nature, and then six months later leadership changes and suddenly the impossible thing is mandatory.\n\nSo… I don’t think I believed anything obviously untrue. At least not that I remember. But if you mean did other people think I was being unrealistic about my plans, maybe, yes, they probably did. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to measure “usual self,” I guess. I mean, day to day I still go to work, still do the grocery run, still answer emails — although email is a terrible measure because nobody is ever really caught up, you know?

My wife might say there are times I’m more settled, maybe less… I don’t know, keyed into everything. But from the inside it’s hard to tell. You just sort of keep moving, and then later someone says, “You were different that month,” and I’m thinking, was I? Or was that just a busy quarter?

So I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I think there are stretches where things feel more normal, but I don’t always notice the transition.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because things kind of blew up at work. I work in HR, and there was this onboarding launch we’d been talking about forever, and I just… pushed it ahead. I committed company money and vendor time before all the approvals were actually in place.\n\nAt the time it felt like the right move, like bold and necessary, you know? Like if I didn’t drive it forward, it would just die in meetings like everything else. But now I’m on leave, and people keep asking me what I was thinking, and honestly I’m not sure how to answer that without sounding ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: During the time you pushed the project ahead, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly. I just wasn’t paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not “happy” exactly, not like vacation happy. More like I felt brilliantly alive, like I could finally show leadership what HR is supposed to be, not just paperwork and policy reminders.\n\nAnd then if someone questioned me — even something small, like an edit to a deck or a routine budget question — I’d get furious. Like, disproportionate furious. Which is strange because normally I can sit through endless feedback meetings, that’s basically half of HR, but during those weeks it felt like everyone was trying to slow me down or make me smaller.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel driven to take on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… it didn’t feel like “a lot” at the time, it felt like finally doing what should have been done already.\n\nI was trying to run the HR redesign, and at the same time I was reaching out to gallery partners — which, I know, sounds unrelated, but there was this idea about making the onboarding experience more connected to local culture, and then that made me think of festivals, so I started contacting festival organizers too. And then there were these staff conflicts that had been sitting around forever, so I was jumping into those like, “I can fix this today.”\n\nIt was like every idea had to be acted on immediately, before anyone could bury it in another committee meeting.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel unusually powerful or especially certain that your ideas were uniquely important?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision. If you don’t sound convinced, then everyone piles on with doubts and suddenly you’re in a meeting about a meeting.\n\nThe “uniquely important” part… I don’t know. I remember feeling like people weren’t seeing the obvious path, but that happens in HR more than people realize. Everyone thinks HR is just forms and complaints, but there’s strategy there, culture, retention, all of it. Like when we changed the performance review templates years ago, everyone acted like it was just a document, but it changed the whole tone of manager conversations.\n\nSo… maybe I felt strongly that my ideas mattered, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually powerful. I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, were you sleeping much less than usual and still feeling full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Sleep is one of those things I only notice after the fact, if that makes sense. I don’t keep track of it very well.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches where I’m up late thinking about work or answering one more email, but I don’t know that I could say, “Yes, I was sleeping much less and felt great the next day.” I’d have to think about it more. My wife would probably have a better read on that than I do — she notices things like whether I’m getting up at weird hours or moving around the house.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no exactly, I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you believe things that other people said were not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “not true” is a hard thing, because in an office people call things unrealistic all the time when what they really mean is inconvenient or not in the budget yet.\n\nLike, I’d suggest something and people would say, “That’s not how we do things,” but that’s not the same as it being false, right? HR is full of that — policies that everyone treats like laws of nature, and then six months later leadership changes and suddenly the impossible thing is mandatory.\n\nSo… I don’t think I believed anything obviously untrue. At least not that I remember. But if you mean did other people think I was being unrealistic about my plans, maybe, yes, they probably did. I’m not sure that’s the same thing though.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period at work, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt unusually energized or driven in a similar way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m trying to think before the work situation, and I don’t know if I’d call it the same, exactly. But there have definitely been stretches where I get this… drive, like I can’t just sit and wait for things to happen.\n\nLike I’ll get in early, before most people are even settled, and I’m already walking the floors, checking in on what teams are doing, looking at conference rooms like I’m mapping a campaign or something. Then I’m back rewriting decks that were probably fine the night before, sending emails to people because some piece of the plan suddenly seems obvious, rearranging timelines, moving things around.\n\nIt’s not even always that I feel stressed. It’s more like my body has a motor in it. Sitting at my desk feels almost impossible, like I should be up fixing something or connecting dots. Which… in HR, that can look productive, honestly. People like when you’re proactive. But yes, I guess there have been times like that.\n\nDoctor: Between these driven periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure “usual self,” I guess. I mean, day to day I still go to work, still do the grocery run, still answer emails — although email is a terrible measure because nobody is ever really caught up, you know?\n\nMy wife might say there are times I’m more settled, maybe less… I don’t know, keyed into everything. I think there are stretches where things feel more normal, but I don’t always notice the transition.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those driven periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / human_resources_worker / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Pressured_Speech\nmanifestation: \"In meetings I can hear myself talking faster and longer than usual, jumping from employee engagement to gallery layouts to retention metrics, and people have to practically raise their hands to stop me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t always notice it in the moment, because in a meeting you’re supposed to be talking, right? Especially HR — half the job is explaining things everyone already half-understands but in a way that doesn’t make anyone panic.

But there are times I can hear myself going faster, like I’ve started on employee engagement, and then suddenly I’m talking about gallery layouts — because the office space affects morale, which affects retention, so it connects — and then I’m on retention metrics and survey design and who needs to be looped in. And I’m still talking.

People kind of sit there waiting for a gap, and sometimes they practically have to raise their hands to get me to stop or ask a question. So… yes, during those periods I probably talk more than usual.

I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly. I just wasn’t paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not “happy” exactly, not like vacation happy. Like, disproportionate furious. Which is strange because normally I can sit through endless feedback meetings, that’s basically half of HR, but during those weeks it felt like everyone was trying to slow me down or make me smaller.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel driven to take on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… it didn’t feel like “a lot” at the time, it felt like finally doing what should have been done already.\n\nI was trying to run the HR redesign, and at the same time I was reaching out to gallery partners — which, I know, sounds unrelated, but there was this idea about making the onboarding experience more connected to local culture, and then that made me think of festivals, so I started contacting festival organizers too. And then there were these staff conflicts that had been sitting around forever, so I was jumping into those like, “I can fix this today.”\n\nIt was like every idea had to be acted on immediately, before anyone could bury it in another committee meeting.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel unusually powerful or especially certain that your ideas were uniquely important?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision. If you don’t sound convinced, then everyone piles on with doubts and suddenly you’re in a meeting about a meeting.\n\nThe “uniquely important” part… I don’t know. I remember feeling like people weren’t seeing the obvious path, but that happens in HR more than people realize. Everyone thinks HR is just forms and complaints, but there’s strategy there, culture, retention, all of it. Like when we changed the performance review templates years ago, everyone acted like it was just a document, but it changed the whole tone of manager conversations.\n\nSo… maybe I felt strongly that my ideas mattered, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually powerful. I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, were you sleeping much less than usual and still feeling full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Sleep is one of those things I only notice after the fact, if that makes sense. I don’t keep track of it very well.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches where I’m up late thinking about work or answering one more email, but I don’t know that I could say, “Yes, I was sleeping much less and felt great the next day.” I’d have to think about it more. My wife would probably have a better read on that than I do — she notices things like whether I’m getting up at weird hours or moving around the house.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no exactly, I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you believe things that other people said were not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “not true” is a hard thing, because in an office people call things unrealistic all the time when what they really mean is inconvenient or not in the budget yet.\n\nLike, I’d suggest something and people would say, “That’s not how we do things,” but that’s not the same as it being false, right? Then I’m back rewriting decks that were probably fine the night before, sending emails to people because some piece of the plan suddenly seems obvious, rearranging timelines, moving things around.\n\nIt’s not even always that I feel stressed. It’s more like my body has a motor in it. Sitting at my desk feels almost impossible, like I should be up fixing something or connecting dots. Which… in HR, that can look productive, honestly. It’s more like my body has a motor in it. Sitting at my desk feels almost impossible, like I should be up fixing something or connecting dots. Which… in HR, that can look productive, honestly. People like when you’re proactive. But yes, I guess there have been times like that.\n\nDoctor: Between these driven periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure “usual self,” I guess. I mean, day to day I still go to work, still do the grocery run, still answer emails — although email is a terrible measure because nobody is ever really caught up, you know?\n\nMy wife might say there are times I’m more settled, maybe less… I don’t know, keyed into everything. But from the inside it’s hard to tell. You just sort of keep moving, and then later someone says, “You were different that month,” and I’m thinking, was I? Or was that just a busy quarter?\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I think there are stretches where things feel more normal, but I don’t always notice the transition.\n\nDoctor: During those driven periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always notice it in the moment, because in a meeting you’re supposed to be talking, right? Especially HR — half the job is explaining things everyone already half-understands but in a way that doesn’t make anyone panic.\n\nBut there are times I can hear myself going faster, like I’ve started on employee engagement, and then suddenly I’m talking about gallery layouts — because the office space affects morale, which affects retention, so it connects — and then I’m on retention metrics and survey design and who needs to be looped in. And I’m still talking.\n\nPeople kind of sit there waiting for a gap, and sometimes they practically have to raise their hands to get me to stop or ask a question. So… yes, during those periods I probably talk more than usual.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these periods, do you become much more active than usual, like constantly starting tasks or moving from one project to another?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / human_resources_worker / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Goal_Activity\nmanifestation: \"I've been trying to run the HR redesign, line up gallery partners, contact festival organizers, and fix several staff conflicts all at once, as if every idea has to be acted on immediately.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s probably one of the clearer things, actually.

I’ll start one thing and then it’s like another piece lights up, and I’m already moving to that. Like I’ve been trying to run this HR redesign, but then also lining up gallery partners, and then I’ll think, oh, we should contact the festival organizers now, not next week, and then there are three staff conflicts that I suddenly feel like I have to fix immediately.

It’s not like I calmly make a list and work through it. It’s more like every idea feels time-sensitive, like if I don’t act on it right then, the whole thing loses momentum. And then I’m bouncing between all of it.

I work in HR, and there was this onboarding launch we’d been talking about forever, and I just… pushed it ahead. I committed company money and vendor time before all the approvals were actually in place.\n\nAt the time it felt like the right move, like bold and necessary, you know? Like if I didn’t drive it forward, it would just die in meetings like everything else. But now I’m on leave, and people keep asking me what I was thinking, and honestly I’m not sure how to answer that without sounding ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: During the time you pushed the project ahead, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly. I just wasn’t paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not “happy” exactly, not like vacation happy. More like I felt brilliantly alive, like I could finally show leadership what HR is supposed to be, not just paperwork and policy reminders.\n\nAnd then if someone questioned me — even something small, like an edit to a deck or a routine budget question — I’d get furious. Like, disproportionate furious. Which is strange because normally I can sit through endless feedback meetings, that’s basically half of HR, but during those weeks it felt like everyone was trying to slow me down or make me smaller.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel driven to take on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… it didn’t feel like “a lot” at the time, it felt like finally doing what should have been done already.\n\nI was trying to run the HR redesign, and at the same time I was reaching out to gallery partners — which, I know, sounds unrelated, but there was this idea about making the onboarding experience more connected to local culture, and then that made me think of festivals, so I started contacting festival organizers too. And then there were these staff conflicts that had been sitting around forever, so I was jumping into those like, “I can fix this today.”\n\nIt was like every idea had to be acted on immediately, before anyone could bury it in another committee meeting.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel unusually powerful or especially certain that your ideas were uniquely important?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision. If you don’t sound convinced, then everyone piles on with doubts and suddenly you’re in a meeting about a meeting.\n\nThe “uniquely important” part… I don’t know. I remember feeling like people weren’t seeing the obvious path, but that happens in HR more than people realize. Everyone thinks HR is just forms and complaints, but there’s strategy there, culture, retention, all of it. Like when we changed the performance review templates years ago, everyone acted like it was just a document, but it changed the whole tone of manager conversations.\n\nSo… maybe I felt strongly that my ideas mattered, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually powerful. I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, were you sleeping much less than usual and still feeling full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Sleep is one of those things I only notice after the fact, if that makes sense. I don’t keep track of it very well.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches where I’m up late thinking about work or answering one more email, but I don’t know that I could say, “Yes, I was sleeping much less and felt great the next day.” I’d have to think about it more. My wife would probably have a better read on that than I do — she notices things like whether I’m getting up at weird hours or moving around the house.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no exactly, I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you believe things that other people said were not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Like if I didn’t drive it forward, it would just die in meetings like everything else. But now I’m on leave, and people keep asking me what I was thinking, and honestly I’m not sure how to answer that without sounding ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: During the time you pushed the project ahead, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly. I just wasn’t paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not “happy” exactly, not like vacation happy. More like I felt brilliantly alive, like I could finally show leadership what HR is supposed to be, not just paperwork and policy reminders.\n\nAnd then if someone questioned me — even something small, like an edit to a deck or a routine budget question — I’d get furious. Like, disproportionate furious. Which is strange because normally I can sit through endless feedback meetings, that’s basically half of HR, but during those weeks it felt like everyone was trying to slow me down or make me smaller.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel driven to take on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… it didn’t feel like “a lot” at the time, it felt like finally doing what should have been done already.\n\nI was trying to run the HR redesign, and at the same time I was reaching out to gallery partners — which, I know, sounds unrelated, but there was this idea about making the onboarding experience more connected to local culture, and then that made me think of festivals, so I started contacting festival organizers too. And then there were these staff conflicts that had been sitting around forever, so I was jumping into those like, “I can fix this today.”\n\nIt was like every idea had to be acted on immediately, before anyone could bury it in another committee meeting.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel unusually powerful or especially certain that your ideas were uniquely important?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision. If you don’t sound convinced, then everyone piles on with doubts and suddenly you’re in a meeting about a meeting.\n\nThe “uniquely important” part… I don’t know. I remember feeling like people weren’t seeing the obvious path, but that happens in HR more than people realize. Everyone thinks HR is just forms and complaints, but there’s strategy there, culture, retention, all of it. Like when we changed the performance review templates years ago, everyone acted like it was just a document, but it changed the whole tone of manager conversations.\n\nSo… maybe I felt strongly that my ideas mattered, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually powerful. I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, were you sleeping much less than usual and still feeling full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Sleep is one of those things I only notice after the fact, if that makes sense. Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: In meetings I can hear myself talking faster and longer than usual, jumping from employee engagement to gallery layouts to retention metrics, and people have to practically raise their hands to stop me.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I'll start reviewing an employee-relations file and then a dashboard color, a Slack ping, or an idea for a display wall pulls me completely off track before I finish the first thing.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I've been trying to run the HR redesign, line up gallery partners, contact festival organizers, and fix several staff conflicts all at once, as if every idea has to be acted on immediately.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: Because of how I handled the launch and the conflicts around it, I’ve been put on administrative leave and I’m no longer trusted to manage the HR projects I was leading.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: This has been building for about a month, since the new executive started challenging my onboarding redesign.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / human_resources_worker / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S041\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: During those driven periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always notice it in the moment, because in a meeting you’re supposed to be talking, right? Especially HR — half the job is explaining things everyone already half-understands but in a way that doesn’t make anyone panic.\n\nBut there are times I can hear myself going faster, like I’ve started on employee engagement, and then suddenly I’m talking about gallery layouts — because the office space affects morale, which affects retention, so it connects — and then I’m on retention metrics and survey design and who needs to be looped in. And I’m still talking.\n\nPeople kind of sit there waiting for a gap, and sometimes they practically have to raise their hands to get me to stop or ask a question. Like I’ve been trying to run this HR redesign, but then also lining up gallery partners, and then I’ll think, oh, we should contact the festival organizers now, not next week, and then there are three staff conflicts that I suddenly feel like I have to fix immediately.\n\nIt’s not like I calmly make a list and work through it. It’s more like every idea feels time-sensitive, like if I don’t act on it right then, the whole thing loses momentum. And then I’m bouncing between all of it.\n\nDoctor: During one of these driven periods, have you ever been hospitalized because of your behavior?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? No, I don’t think so. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “More important” sounds… I don’t know, like I’m walking around thinking I’m royalty or something, and I don’t think that’s me.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because things kind of blew up at work. I work in HR, and there was this onboarding launch we’d been talking about forever, and I just… pushed it ahead. I committed company money and vendor time before all the approvals were actually in place.\n\nAt the time it felt like the right move, like bold and necessary, you know? Like if I didn’t drive it forward, it would just die in meetings like everything else. But now I’m on leave, and people keep asking me what I was thinking, and honestly I’m not sure how to answer that without sounding ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: During the time you pushed the project ahead, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly. I just wasn’t paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not “happy” exactly, not like vacation happy. More like I felt brilliantly alive, like I could finally show leadership what HR is supposed to be, not just paperwork and policy reminders.\n\nAnd then if someone questioned me — even something small, like an edit to a deck or a routine budget question — I’d get furious. Like, disproportionate furious. Which is strange because normally I can sit through endless feedback meetings, that’s basically half of HR, but during those weeks it felt like everyone was trying to slow me down or make me smaller.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel driven to take on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… it didn’t feel like “a lot” at the time, it felt like finally doing what should have been done already.\n\nI was trying to run the HR redesign, and at the same time I was reaching out to gallery partners — which, I know, sounds unrelated, but there was this idea about making the onboarding experience more connected to local culture, and then that made me think of festivals, so I started contacting festival organizers too. And then there were these staff conflicts that had been sitting around forever, so I was jumping into those like, “I can fix this today.”\n\nIt was like every idea had to be acted on immediately, before anyone could bury it in another committee meeting.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel unusually powerful or especially certain that your ideas were uniquely important?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision. If you don’t sound convinced, then everyone piles on with doubts and suddenly you’re in a meeting about a meeting.\n\nThe “uniquely important” part… I don’t know. I remember feeling like people weren’t seeing the obvious path, but that happens in HR more than people realize. Everyone thinks HR is just forms and complaints, but there’s strategy there, culture, retention, all of it. Like when we changed the performance review templates years ago, everyone acted like it was just a document, but it changed the whole tone of manager conversations.\n\nSo… maybe I felt strongly that my ideas mattered, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually powerful. I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, were you sleeping much less than usual and still feeling full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Sleep is one of those things I only notice after the fact, if that makes sense. I don’t keep track of it very well.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches where I’m up late thinking about work or answering one more email, but I don’t know that I could say, “Yes, I was sleeping much less and felt great the next day.” I’d have to think about it more. My wife would probably have a better read on that than I do — she notices things like whether I’m getting up at weird hours or moving around the house.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no exactly, I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you believe things that other people said were not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “not true” is a hard thing, because in an office people call things unrealistic all the time when what they really mean is inconvenient or not in the budget yet.\n\nLike, I’d suggest something and people would say, “That’s not how we do things,” but that’s not the same as it being false, right? HR is full of that — policies that everyone treats like laws of nature, and then six months later leadership changes and suddenly the impossible thing is mandatory.\n\nSo… I don’t think I believed anything obviously untrue. At least not that I remember. But if you mean did other people think I was being unrealistic about my plans, maybe, yes, they probably did. I’m not sure that’s the same thing though.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period at work, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt unusually energized or driven in a similar way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m trying to think before the work situation, and I don’t know if I’d call it the same, exactly. But there have definitely been stretches where I get this… drive, like I can’t just sit and wait for things to happen.\n\nLike I’ll get in early, before most people are even settled, and I’m already walking the floors, checking in on what teams are doing, looking at conference rooms like I’m mapping a campaign or something. Then I’m back rewriting decks that were probably fine the night before, sending emails to people because some piece of the plan suddenly seems obvious, rearranging timelines, moving things around.\n\nIt’s not even always that I feel stressed. It’s more like my body has a motor in it. Sitting at my desk feels almost impossible, like I should be up fixing something or connecting dots. Which… in HR, that can look productive, honestly. People like when you’re proactive. But yes, I guess there have been times like that.\n\nDoctor: Between these driven periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure “usual self,” I guess. I mean, day to day I still go to work, still do the grocery run, still answer emails — although email is a terrible measure because nobody is ever really caught up, you know?\n\nMy wife might say there are times I’m more settled, maybe less… I don’t know, keyed into everything. But from the inside it’s hard to tell. You just sort of keep moving, and then later someone says, “You were different that month,” and I’m thinking, was I? Or was that just a busy quarter?\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I think there are stretches where things feel more normal, but I don’t always notice the transition.\n\nDoctor: During those driven periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always notice it in the moment, because in a meeting you’re supposed to be talking, right? I committed company money and vendor time before all the approvals were actually in place.\n\nAt the time it felt like the right move, like bold and necessary, you know? Like if I didn’t drive it forward, it would just die in meetings like everything else. But now I’m on leave, and people keep asking me what I was thinking, and honestly I’m not sure how to answer that without sounding ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: During the time you pushed the project ahead, were you needing much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly. I just wasn’t paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, not “happy” exactly, not like vacation happy. More like I felt brilliantly alive, like I could finally show leadership what HR is supposed to be, not just paperwork and policy reminders.\n\nAnd then if someone questioned me — even something small, like an edit to a deck or a routine budget question — I’d get furious. Like, disproportionate furious. Which is strange because normally I can sit through endless feedback meetings, that’s basically half of HR, but during those weeks it felt like everyone was trying to slow me down or make me smaller.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel driven to take on a lot more projects or activities than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean… it didn’t feel like “a lot” at the time, it felt like finally doing what should have been done already.\n\nI was trying to run the HR redesign, and at the same time I was reaching out to gallery partners — which, I know, sounds unrelated, but there was this idea about making the onboarding experience more connected to local culture, and then that made me think of festivals, so I started contacting festival organizers too. And then there were these staff conflicts that had been sitting around forever, so I was jumping into those like, “I can fix this today.”\n\nIt was like every idea had to be acted on immediately, before anyone could bury it in another committee meeting.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel unusually powerful or especially certain that your ideas were uniquely important?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision. If you don’t sound convinced, then everyone piles on with doubts and suddenly you’re in a meeting about a meeting.\n\nThe “uniquely important” part… I don’t know. I remember feeling like people weren’t seeing the obvious path, but that happens in HR more than people realize. Everyone thinks HR is just forms and complaints, but there’s strategy there, culture, retention, all of it. Like when we changed the performance review templates years ago, everyone acted like it was just a document, but it changed the whole tone of manager conversations.\n\nSo… maybe I felt strongly that my ideas mattered, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually powerful. I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, were you sleeping much less than usual and still feeling full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Sleep is one of those things I only notice after the fact, if that makes sense. I don’t keep track of it very well.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches where I’m up late thinking about work or answering one more email, but I don’t know that I could say, “Yes, I was sleeping much less and felt great the next day.” I’d have to think about it more. My wife would probably have a better read on that than I do — she notices things like whether I’m getting up at weird hours or moving around the house.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no exactly, I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you believe things that other people said were not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “not true” is a hard thing, because in an office people call things unrealistic all the time when what they really mean is inconvenient or not in the budget yet.\n\nLike, I’d suggest something and people would say, “That’s not how we do things,” but that’s not the same as it being false, right? HR is full of that — policies that everyone treats like laws of nature, and then six months later leadership changes and suddenly the impossible thing is mandatory.\n\nSo… I don’t think I believed anything obviously untrue. At least not that I remember. But if you mean did other people think I was being unrealistic about my plans, maybe, yes, they probably did. I’m not sure that’s the same thing though.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period at work, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt unusually energized or driven in a similar way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m trying to think before the work situation, and I don’t know if I’d call it the same, exactly. But there have definitely been stretches where I get this… drive, like I can’t just sit and wait for things to happen.\n\nLike I’ll get in early, before most people are even settled, and I’m already walking the floors, checking in on what teams are doing, looking at conference rooms like I’m mapping a campaign or something. Then I’m back rewriting decks that were probably fine the night before, sending emails to people because some piece of the plan suddenly seems obvious, rearranging timelines, moving things around.\n\nIt’s not even always that I feel stressed. It’s more like my body has a motor in it. Sitting at my desk feels almost impossible, like I should be up fixing something or connecting dots. Which… in HR, that can look productive, honestly. People like when you’re proactive. But yes, I guess there have been times like that.\n\nDoctor: Between these driven periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure “usual self,” I guess. I mean, day to day I still go to work, still do the grocery run, still answer emails — although email is a terrible measure because nobody is ever really caught up, you know?\n\nMy wife might say there are times I’m more settled, maybe less… I don’t know, keyed into everything. But from the inside it’s hard to tell. You just sort of keep moving, and then later someone says, “You were different that month,” and I’m thinking, was I? Or was that just a busy quarter?\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I think there are stretches where things feel more normal, but I don’t always notice the transition.\n\nDoctor: During those driven periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always notice it in the moment, because in a meeting you’re supposed to be talking, right? Especially HR — half the job is explaining things everyone already half-understands but in a way that doesn’t make anyone panic.\n\nBut there are times I can hear myself going faster, like I’ve started on employee engagement, and then suddenly I’m talking about gallery layouts — because the office space affects morale, which affects retention, so it connects — and then I’m on retention metrics and survey design and who needs to be looped in. And I’m still talking.\n\nPeople kind of sit there waiting for a gap, and sometimes they practically have to raise their hands to get me to stop or ask a question. So… yes, during those periods I probably talk more than usual.\n\nDoctor: During these periods, do you become much more active than usual, like constantly starting tasks or moving from one project to another?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the clearer things, actually.\n\nI’ll start one thing and then it’s like another piece lights up, and I’m already moving to that. Like I’ve been trying to run this HR redesign, but then also lining up gallery partners, and then I’ll think, oh, we should contact the festival organizers now, not next week, and then there are three staff conflicts that I suddenly feel like I have to fix immediately.\n\nIt’s not like I calmly make a list and work through it. It’s more like every idea feels time-sensitive, like if I don’t act on it right then, the whole thing loses momentum. And then I’m bouncing between all of it.\n\nDoctor: During one of these driven periods, have you ever been hospitalized because of your behavior?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? No, I don’t think so. I mean, not in the sense of being admitted or kept somewhere because of how I was acting.\n\nThere was one time my wife said I should “talk to someone before this turns into a thing,” but that’s not the same as a hospital. And honestly, hospitals make me think of my dad’s knee surgery, that whole fluorescent-light waiting room feeling, vending machine coffee… sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: During those energized periods, do you feel more important or more capable than other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “More important” sounds… I don’t know, like I’m walking around thinking I’m royalty or something, and I don’t think that’s me.\n\nMore capable, maybe I feel like I can see the whole board at once, if that makes sense. But then again, in HR you kind of have to act like you know what you’re doing even when everyone’s looking at you to explain a policy that was written by someone in legal ten years ago. I remember this one benefits meeting where the projector wouldn’t connect, and somehow I ended up troubleshooting cables while also talking through open enrollment, and people were joking that I should run IT too—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I don’t really know. I guess I haven’t noticed myself thinking I’m better than other people, exactly. More like I feel… very sure I can handle things.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, have other people clearly told you that you were acting unlike yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people have said something like that. My wife especially. She’ll say, “You’re not yourself right now,” usually after I’ve gotten really intense about something that probably didn’t need to be that intense.\n\nAt work too, not always directly, but I can tell. Like for the past few weeks I’ve felt… I don’t know, brilliantly alive sometimes, like I can finally show leadership what HR is supposed to be, not just forms and compliance and awkward trainings in a conference room with stale muffins. And then if someone questions me — even a small edit, or a routine budget question — I can just snap into being furious, like they’re trying to sabotage the whole thing.\n\nSo yes, people have made it clear I’m acting different. I don’t always agree in the moment, but later I can see why they said it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Then I’m back rewriting decks that were probably fine the night before, sending emails to people because some piece of the plan suddenly seems obvious, rearranging timelines, moving things around.\n\nIt’s not even always that I feel stressed. It’s more like my body has a motor in it. Sitting at my desk feels almost impossible, like I should be up fixing something or connecting dots. Which… in HR, that can look productive, honestly. People like when you’re proactive. But yes, I guess there have been times like that.\n\nDoctor: Between these driven periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure “usual self,” I guess. I mean, day to day I still go to work, still do the grocery run, still answer emails — although email is a terrible measure because nobody is ever really caught up, you know?\n\nMy wife might say there are times I’m more settled, maybe less… I don’t know, keyed into everything. But from the inside it’s hard to tell. You just sort of keep moving, and then later someone says, “You were different that month,” and I’m thinking, was I? Or was that just a busy quarter?\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I think there are stretches where things feel more normal, but I don’t always notice the transition.\n\nDoctor: During those driven periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always notice it in the moment, because in a meeting you’re supposed to be talking, right? Especially HR — half the job is explaining things everyone already half-understands but in a way that doesn’t make anyone panic.\n\nBut there are times I can hear myself going faster, like I’ve started on employee engagement, and then suddenly I’m talking about gallery layouts — because the office space affects morale, which affects retention, so it connects — and then I’m on retention metrics and survey design and who needs to be looped in. And I’m still talking.\n\nPeople kind of sit there waiting for a gap, and sometimes they practically have to raise their hands to get me to stop or ask a question. So… yes, during those periods I probably talk more than usual.\n\nDoctor: During these periods, do you become much more active than usual, like constantly starting tasks or moving from one project to another?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the clearer things, actually.\n\nI’ll start one thing and then it’s like another piece lights up, and I’m already moving to that. Like I’ve been trying to run this HR redesign, but then also lining up gallery partners, and then I’ll think, oh, we should contact the festival organizers now, not next week, and then there are three staff conflicts that I suddenly feel like I have to fix immediately.\n\nIt’s not like I calmly make a list and work through it. It’s more like every idea feels time-sensitive, like if I don’t act on it right then, the whole thing loses momentum. And then I’m bouncing between all of it.\n\nDoctor: During one of these driven periods, have you ever been hospitalized because of your behavior?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? No, I don’t think so. But then again, in HR you kind of have to act like you know what you’re doing even when everyone’s looking at you to explain a policy that was written by someone in legal ten years ago. I remember this one benefits meeting where the projector wouldn’t connect, and somehow I ended up troubleshooting cables while also talking through open enrollment, and people were joking that I should run IT too—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I don’t really know. I guess I haven’t noticed myself thinking I’m better than other people, exactly. More like I feel… very sure I can handle things.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, have other people clearly told you that you were acting unlike yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people have said something like that. My wife especially. She’ll say, “You’re not yourself right now,” usually after I’ve gotten really intense about something that probably didn’t need to be that intense.\n\nAt work too, not always directly, but I can tell. Like for the past few weeks I’ve felt… I don’t know, brilliantly alive sometimes, like I can finally show leadership what HR is supposed to be, not just forms and compliance and awkward trainings in a conference room with stale muffins. And then if someone questions me — even a small edit, or a routine budget question — I can just snap into being furious, like they’re trying to sabotage the whole thing.\n\nSo yes, people have made it clear I’m acting different. I don’t always agree in the moment, but later I can see why they said it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these energized periods that lasted at least a full week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely at least a week. This current one has been going on longer than that, honestly.\n\nIt’s been building for about a month now, since the new executive started pushing back on my onboarding redesign. Which sounds so boring when I say it out loud — onboarding, forms, welcome emails, who gets a badge on day one — but it’s not just that. It’s like she questioned one piece of it, and then suddenly the whole thing became this… I don’t know, campaign in my head.\n\nSorry, yes — to answer your actual question, yes. More than a week. About a month.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. About a month.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / human_resources_worker / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can say “nearly every day” in that exact way. I’ve had stretches where I felt really low, sure, but two full weeks where it was most of the day every day… I’m not sure.

Part of me wants to say yes because everyone gets ground down eventually, especially when work is just this constant stream of people’s problems—complaints, benefits issues, someone crying in my office because their manager is awful—and you carry that home even when you pretend you don’t. But that’s not the same thing as what you’re asking, probably.

So… I don’t know. I can’t clearly point to a two-week period like that.

I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly. I mean… it didn’t feel like “a lot” at the time, it felt like finally doing what should have been done already.\n\nI was trying to run the HR redesign, and at the same time I was reaching out to gallery partners — which, I know, sounds unrelated, but there was this idea about making the onboarding experience more connected to local culture, and then that made me think of festivals, so I started contacting festival organizers too. And then there were these staff conflicts that had been sitting around forever, so I was jumping into those like, “I can fix this today.”\n\nIt was like every idea had to be acted on immediately, before anyone could bury it in another committee meeting.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel unusually powerful or especially certain that your ideas were uniquely important?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. “Powerful” sounds a little dramatic, doesn’t it? I mean, I was confident, maybe very confident, but at work you’re sort of expected to sell the vision. If you don’t sound convinced, then everyone piles on with doubts and suddenly you’re in a meeting about a meeting.\n\nThe “uniquely important” part… I don’t know. I remember feeling like people weren’t seeing the obvious path, but that happens in HR more than people realize. Everyone thinks HR is just forms and complaints, but there’s strategy there, culture, retention, all of it. Like when we changed the performance review templates years ago, everyone acted like it was just a document, but it changed the whole tone of manager conversations.\n\nSo… maybe I felt strongly that my ideas mattered, but I don’t know if I’d call it unusually powerful. I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During that time, were you sleeping much less than usual and still feeling full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Sleep is one of those things I only notice after the fact, if that makes sense. I don’t keep track of it very well.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches where I’m up late thinking about work or answering one more email, but I don’t know that I could say, “Yes, I was sleeping much less and felt great the next day.” I’d have to think about it more. My wife would probably have a better read on that than I do — she notices things like whether I’m getting up at weird hours or moving around the house.\n\nSo… I’m not saying no exactly, I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you believe things that other people said were not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “not true” is a hard thing, because in an office people call things unrealistic all the time when what they really mean is inconvenient or not in the budget yet.\n\nLike, I’d suggest something and people would say, “That’s not how we do things,” but that’s not the same as it being false, right? HR is full of that — policies that everyone treats like laws of nature, and then six months later leadership changes and suddenly the impossible thing is mandatory.\n\nSo… I don’t think I believed anything obviously untrue. At least not that I remember. But if you mean did other people think I was being unrealistic about my plans, maybe, yes, they probably did. I’m not sure that’s the same thing though.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period at work, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt unusually energized or driven in a similar way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m trying to think before the work situation, and I don’t know if I’d call it the same, exactly. But there have definitely been stretches where I get this… drive, like I can’t just sit and wait for things to happen.\n\nLike I’ll get in early, before most people are even settled, and I’m already walking the floors, checking in on what teams are doing, looking at conference rooms like I’m mapping a campaign or something. Then I’m back rewriting decks that were probably fine the night before, sending emails to people because some piece of the plan suddenly seems obvious, rearranging timelines, moving things around.\n\nIt’s not even always that I feel stressed. It’s more like my body has a motor in it. Sitting at my desk feels almost impossible, like I should be up fixing something or connecting dots. Which… in HR, that can look productive, honestly. People like when you’re proactive. But yes, I guess there have been times like that.\n\nDoctor: Between these driven periods, do you return to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to measure “usual self,” I guess. I mean, day to day I still go to work, still do the grocery run, still answer emails — although email is a terrible measure because nobody is ever really caught up, you know?\n\nMy wife might say there are times I’m more settled, maybe less… I don’t know, keyed into everything. But from the inside it’s hard to tell. You just sort of keep moving, and then later someone says, “You were different that month,” and I’m thinking, was I? Or was that just a busy quarter?\n\nSo I don’t have a clean yes-or-no answer. I think there are stretches where things feel more normal, but I don’t always notice the transition.\n\nDoctor: During those driven periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always notice it in the moment, because in a meeting you’re supposed to be talking, right? Especially HR — half the job is explaining things everyone already half-understands but in a way that doesn’t make anyone panic.\n\nBut there are times I can hear myself going faster, like I’ve started on employee engagement, and then suddenly I’m talking about gallery layouts — because the office space affects morale, which affects retention, so it connects — and then I’m on retention metrics and survey design and who needs to be looped in. And I’m still talking.\n\nPeople kind of sit there waiting for a gap, and sometimes they practically have to raise their hands to get me to stop or ask a question. So… yes, during those periods I probably talk more than usual.\n\nDoctor: During these periods, do you become much more active than usual, like constantly starting tasks or moving from one project to another?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the clearer things, actually.\n\nI’ll start one thing and then it’s like another piece lights up, and I’m already moving to that. Like I’ve been trying to run this HR redesign, but then also lining up gallery partners, and then I’ll think, oh, we should contact the festival organizers now, not next week, and then there are three staff conflicts that I suddenly feel like I have to fix immediately.\n\nIt’s not like I calmly make a list and work through it. It’s more like every idea feels time-sensitive, like if I don’t act on it right then, the whole thing loses momentum. And then I’m bouncing between all of it.\n\nDoctor: During one of these driven periods, have you ever been hospitalized because of your behavior?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? No, I don’t think so. I mean, that whole stretch is kind of blurry because the launch was taking over everything, and I’d be up thinking about vendor timelines or whether Legal was going to drag their feet again, and then I’d remember some completely unrelated thing, like this one compliance training rollout from years ago where everyone blamed HR for delays even though it wasn’t really us.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I needed less sleep exactly.
